<think>
First, I recognize that the problem involves finding the equation of a parabola given its vertex and focus. The vertex is at (0, 0), and the focus is at (0, 4).

Since both the vertex and focus lie on the y-axis, this indicates that the parabola opens upward.

The standard form of a parabola with a vertical axis and vertex at the origin is \( y = \frac{1}{4p}x^2 \), where \( p \) represents the distance from the vertex to the focus.

Given that the focus is 4 units above the vertex, \( p = 4 \).

Substituting this value into the equation, we get:
\( y = \frac{1}{4 \times 4}x^2 = \frac{1}{16}x^2 \)

Therefore, the equation of the parabola is \( y = \frac{1}{16}x^2 \).

To find the equation of a parabola with a vertex at (0, 0) and a focus at (0, 4), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Identify the Direction of the Parabola
Since both the vertex (0, 0) and the focus (0, 4) lie on the y-axis:
- The parabola **opens upward**.

### Step 2: Use the Standard Form of a Parabola's Equation
For a parabola that opens upward with its vertex at the origin (0, 0), the standard form is:
\[ y = \frac{1}{4p}x^2 \]
where \( p \) is the distance from the vertex to the focus.

### Step 3: Determine the Value of \( p \)
Given that the focus is at (0, 4):
- \( p = 4 \)

### Step 4: Substitute \( p \) into the Equation
Substitute \( p = 4 \) into the standard form:
\[ y = \frac{1}{4 \times 4}x^2 \]
\[ y = \frac{1}{16}x^2 \]

### Final Answer

The equation of the parabola is:
\[
\boxed{\,y = \dfrac{1}{16}\,x^{2}\,}
\]

To convert the given quadratic equation \( x^2 - 4x + 8y + 12 = 0 \) into vertex form, I'll follow these steps:

First, I want to isolate the y-term. I'll move the other terms to the other side of the equation:
\( 8y = -x^2 + 4x - 12 \)

\( y = \frac{-x^2 + 4x - 12}{8} \)

Now, I'll rewrite the equation in a form that's easier to work with:
\( y = -\frac{1}{8}x^2 + \frac{1}{2}x - \frac{3}{2} \)

To convert this into vertex form \( y = a(x - h)^2 + k \), I'll complete the square for the x-terms. 

I factor out the coefficient of \( x^2 \) from the first two terms:
\( y = -\frac{1}{8}(x^2 - 4x) - \frac{3}{2} \)

Now, I'll complete the square inside the parentheses. I take half of the coefficient of x, which is -2, and square it to get 4. I add and subtract this value inside the parentheses:
\( y = -\frac{1}{8}(x^2 - 4x + 4 - 4) - \frac{3}{2} \)

\( y = -\frac{1}{8}((x - 2)^2 - 4) - \frac{3}{2} \)

\( y = -\frac{1}{8}(x - 2)^2 + \frac{1}{2} - \frac{3}{2} \)

Finally, I'll combine the constant terms:

To convert the given quadratic equation into **vertex form**, follow these steps:

**Given Equation:**
\[ x^2 - 4x + 8y + 12 = 0 \]

**Step 1:** Isolate the \( y \)-term.
\[
\begin{align*}
x^2 - 4x + 8y + 12 &= 0 \\
8y &= -x^2 + 4x - 12
\end{align*}
\]

**Step 2:** Solve for \( y \) by dividing each term by 8.
\[
y = \frac{-x^2 + 4x - 12}{8}
\]
\[
y = -\frac{1}{8}x^2 + \frac{1}{2}x - \frac{3}{2}
\]

**Step 3:** Rewrite the equation to complete the square.
\[
y = -\frac{1}{8}(x^2 - 4x) - \frac{3}{2}
\]

**Step 4:** Complete the square inside the parentheses. Take half of the coefficient of \( x \), which is \(-2\), and square it to get 4. Add and subtract this value inside the parentheses.
\[
y = -\frac{1}{8}(x^2 - 4x + 4 - 4) - \frac{3}{2}
\]

**Step 5:** Factor the perfect square trinomial and simplify.
\[
y = -\frac{1}{8}((x - 2)^2 - 4) - \frac{3}{2}
\]
\[
y = -\frac{1}{8}(x - 2)^2 + \frac{1}{2} - \frac{3}{2}
\]

**Step 6:** Combine the constant terms.
\[
y = -\frac{1}{8}(x - 2)^2 - 1
\]

**Final Answer:**
The vertex form of the equation is:
\[
\boxed{y = -\frac{1}{8}(x - 2)^2 - 1}
\]

The given conditions result in a degenerate conic section, specifically the vertical line \(x = 0\). 

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Understand the Definitions:**
   - A parabola is defined as the set of all points equidistant from a fixed point (focus) and a fixed line (directrix).

2. **Given Data:**
   - Focus at \((0, 3)\)
   - Directrix \(y = 3\)

3. **Distance Calculations:**
   - For any point \((x, y)\), the distance to the focus is:
     \[
     \sqrt{(x - 0)^2 + (y - 3)^2} = \sqrt{x^2 + (y - 3)^2}
     \]
   - The distance from \((x, y)\) to the directrix \(y = 3\) is vertical and calculated as:
     \[
     |y - 3|
     \]

4. **Set Up the Equation:**
   - Equate the two distances for points on the parabola:
     \[
     \sqrt{x^2 + (y - 3)^2} = |y - 3|
     \]
   
5. **Square Both Sides to Eliminate the Square Root:**
   \[
   x^2 + (y - 3)^2 = (y - 3)^2
   \]

6. **Simplify the Equation:**
   Subtract \((y - 3)^2\) from both sides:
   \[
   x^2 = 0
   \]
   
7. **Conclusion:**
   The equation \(x^2 = 0\) simplifies to \(x = 0\), which represents a vertical line passing through the focus at \((0, 3)\). 

8. **Implications:**
   Since all points on this line satisfy the condition of being equidistant from the focus and directrix, it forms a degenerate parabola—a straight line.

**Final Answer:**

The equation simplifies to \(x = 0\), which is a vertical line passing through \((0, 3)\). Therefore, there is no non-degenerate parabola with these properties; instead, the result is the line:

\[
\boxed{x = 0}
\]

Finally, to find the focus, I use the formula for a parabola's focus when it's in this form. The value of 'a' is 1/4p, so p = 1/(4a). Calculating p gives me 1/4. With the vertex at (h, k), adding p to the y-coordinate provides the location of the focus.

**Solution:**

We are given the equation of a parabola:

\[ y = (x - 1)^2 + 5 \]

To determine the **focus**, follow these steps:

1. **Identify the Vertex Form:**
   
   The standard form of a parabola's equation that opens upward or downward is:
   
   \[ y = a(x - h)^2 + k \]
   
   where \( (h, k) \) is the vertex.

   Comparing with our given equation:
   
   \[ y = (x - 1)^2 + 5 \]
   
   We have:
   
   \[
   h = 1 \\
   k = 5
   \]
   
   So, the **vertex** of the parabola is at \( (1, 5) \).

2. **Determine the Direction and Value of \( a \):**
   
   In our equation:
   
   \[ y = (x - 1)^2 + 5 \]
   
   The coefficient \( a = 1 \). Since \( a > 0 \), the parabola opens **upwards**.

3. **Find the Focus:**
   
   For a parabola in the form \( y = a(x - h)^2 + k \), the focus is located at:
   
   \[
   (h, k + \frac{1}{4a})
   \]
   
   Plugging in our values:
   
   \[
   a = 1 \\
   \frac{1}{4a} = \frac{1}{4}
   \]
   
   Therefore, the focus is at:
   
   \[
   (1, 5 + \frac{1}{4}) = \left(1, \frac{21}{4}\right)
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\[\boxed{\left\{ \text{Final Answer: } "1", \text{ }\frac{21}{4}" \right\}}\]

Using the general form of a quadratic function \( y = ax^2 + bx + c \), we set up equations based on each point. To determine the equation of a parabola that opens to the right with its vertex at \((-4, 1)\) and focus at \((-2, 1)\), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Recall the Standard Form
The standard form of a parabola that opens to the right is:
\[
(y - k)^2 = 4p(x - h)
\]
where:
- \((h, k)\) is the vertex.
- \(p\) is the distance from the vertex to the focus.

### Step 2: Identify the Known Values
From the problem:
- **Vertex** (\(h, k\)) = \((-4, 1)\)
- **Focus** is at \((-2, 1)\)

### Step 3: Calculate \(p\)
The distance from the vertex to the focus (\(p\)) is calculated as:
\[
p = \text{Focus}_x - \text{Vertex}_x
\]
Substitute the known values:
\[
p = (-2) - (-4) = 2
\]

### Step 4: Plug Values into the Standard Form
Substitute \(h = -4\), \(k = 1\), and \(p = 2\) into the standard form equation:
\[
(y - 1)^2 = 4 \times 2 \times (x - (-4))
\]
Simplify:
\[
(y - 1)^2 = 8(x + 4)
\]

### Final Answer
The equation of the parabola is:

\]

For the y-coordinate:
\[
\frac{1 + (-3)}{2} = -1
\]

\[

\]

\[
r = \frac{d}{2} = \frac{4\sqrt{5}}{2} = 2\sqrt{5}
\]

To determine the equation of a circle with endpoints of its diameter at points \( A(-2, 1) \) and \( B(6, -3) \), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Find the Center of the Circle
The center (\( C(h, k) \)) of the circle is the midpoint of the diameter. The midpoint formula is:
\[
h = \frac{x_1 + x_2}{2}, \quad k = \frac{y_1 + y_2}{2}
\]

Substituting the given points:
\[
h = \frac{-2 + 6}{2} = \frac{4}{2} = 2
\]
\[
k = \frac{1 + (-3)}{2} = \frac{-2}{2} = -1
\]
So, the center is \( (2, -1) \).

### Step 2: Calculate the Radius of the Circle
The radius (\( r \)) is half the length of the diameter. First, find the distance between points \( A \) and \( B \) using the distance formula:
\[
d = \sqrt{(x_2 - x_1)^2 + (y_2 - y_1)^2}
\]
\[
d = \sqrt{(6 - (-2))^2 + (-3 - 1)^2} = \sqrt{8^2 + (-4)^2} = \sqrt{64 + 16} = \sqrt{80} = 4\sqrt{5}
\]
The radius is half of this distance:
\[
r = \frac{d}{2} = \frac{4\sqrt{5}}{2} = 2\sqrt{5}
\]

### Step 3: Write the Equation of the Circle
The standard equation of a circle with center \( (h, k) \) and radius \( r \) is:
\[
(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
\]
Substituting the known values:
\[
(x - 2)^2 + (y - (-1))^2 = (2\sqrt{5})^2
\]
Simplify:
\[
(x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 4 \times 5 = 20
\]

### Final Answer
The equation of the circle is:
\[
\boxed{(x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 20}
\]

   \( x^2 + y^2 - 4x + 2y - 11 = 0 \)

   \( (x^2 - 4x) + (y^2 + 2y) = 11 \)

3. **Complete the square for x:**
   - Take half of -4, which is -2.

   - Take half of 2, which is 1.
   - Square it: (1)^2 = 1
   - Add and subtract 1 inside the equation.

6. **Simplify both sides:**
   \( (x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 16 \)

To determine the equation of a circle that is tangent to the x-axis and has its center at \((3, 4)\), follow these steps:

1. **Understand the Standard Form of a Circle's Equation**:
   
   The general equation of a circle with center \((h, k)\) and radius \(r\) is:
   
   \[
   (x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
   \]
   
2. **Identify the Given Information**:
   
   - Center of the circle: \((3, 4)\), so \(h = 3\) and \(k = 4\).
   - The circle is tangent to the x-axis.

3. **Determine the Radius**:
   
   Since the circle is tangent to the x-axis, the radius must be equal to the y-coordinate of the center (the distance from the center to the x-axis). Therefore:
   
   \[
   r = 4
   \]

   \[

To determine the equation of a circle centered at the origin that passes through the point (0, 5), I first recall the standard form of a circle's equation.

The general equation is \( x^2 + y^2 = r^2 \), where \( r \) represents the radius of the circle. Since the center of this circle is at the origin (0, 0), there are no shifts in the x or y coordinates; thus, the equation remains as simple as possible.

Next, I need to find the value of \( r \). Given that the circle passes through the point (0, 5), I can substitute these values into the equation. Plugging in \( x = 0 \) and \( y = 5 \):

\( 0^2 + 5^2 = r^2 \)

Simplifying this:

\( 0 + 25 = r^2 \)

So, \( r^2 = 25 \). To find the radius \( r \), I take the square root of both sides:

\( r = \sqrt{25} \)
\( r = 5 \)

Now that I have determined the radius to be 5, I can substitute this back into the standard equation of the circle:

\( x^2 + y^2 = 5^2 \)
\( x^2 + y^2 = 25 \)

Therefore, the equation of the circle with its center at the origin and passing through (0, 5) is \( x^2 + y^2 = 25 \).

To find the equation of a circle centered at the origin that passes through the point (0, 5), we can use the standard form of a circle's equation:

\[
x^2 + y^2 = r^2
\]

Here, \( (h, k) = (0, 0) \) is the center of the circle since it's at the origin.

**Step 1: Substitute the given point into the equation to find the radius.**

Given that the circle passes through (0, 5), we substitute \( x = 0 \) and \( y = 5 \):

\[
0^2 + 5^2 = r^2 \\
25 = r^2
\]

**Step 2: Solve for the radius (\( r \)).**

Taking the square root of both sides:

\[
r = \sqrt{25} \\
r = 5
\]

**Step 3: Write the equation of the circle using the found radius.**

Substituting \( r = 5 \) back into the standard equation:

\[
x^2 + y^2 = 5^2 \\
x^2 + y^2 = 25
\]

Thus, the equation of the circle is:

\[
\boxed{x^2 + y^2 = 25}

   r = \sqrt{25} = 5
   \]

4. **Final Answer:**

   - **Center:** \((1, -2)\)
   - **Radius:** \(5\) units

To determine the points of intersection between the line \( y = x + 3 \) and the circle \( x^2 + y^2 = 17 \), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Substitute the Expression for \( y \) from the Line into the Circle's Equation
The given equations are:
\[
y = x + 3 \quad \text{(Line)}
\]
\[
x^2 + y^2 = 17 \quad \text{(Circle)}
\]

Substitute \( y = x + 3 \) into the circle equation:
\[
x^2 + (x + 3)^2 = 17
\]

### Step 2: Expand and Simplify the Equation
Expand \( (x + 3)^2 \):
\[
(x + 3)^2 = x^2 + 6x + 9
\]
So,
\[
x^2 + x^2 + 6x + 9 = 17 \\
2x^2 + 6x + 9 = 17
\]

Subtract 17 from both sides to set the equation to zero:
\[
2x^2 + 6x + 9 - 17 = 0 \\
2x^2 + 6x - 8 = 0
\]

### Step 3: Simplify the Quadratic Equation
Divide the entire equation by 2 to simplify:
\[
x^2 + 3x - 4 = 0
\]

### Step 4: Factor the Quadratic Equation
Find two numbers that multiply to \(-4\) and add up to \(3\). These numbers are \(4\) and \(-1\):
\[
(x + 4)(x - 1) = 0
\]

Set each factor equal to zero:
\[
x + 4 = 0 \quad \Rightarrow \quad x = -4 \\
x - 1 = 0 \quad \Rightarrow \quad x = 1
\]

### Step 5: Find the Corresponding \( y \) Values
Substitute each \( x \) back into \( y = x + 3 \):

- For \( x = -4 \):
\[
y = (-4) + 3 = -1
\]
So, one point is \((-4, -1)\).

- For \( x = 1 \):
\[
y = (1) + 3 = 4
\]
So, the other point is \((1, 4)\).

### Final Answer

The points of intersection are:
\[
\boxed{\{-4, \; -1\}, \{1, \; 4\}}
\]

The given circle equation can be rewritten in standard form by completing the square for both x and y terms. 

1. Start with the original equation:
   \( x^2 + y^2 - 8x - 6y + 20 = 0 \)

2. Rearrange the terms:
   \( (x^2 - 8x) + (y^2 - 6y) = -20 \)

3. Complete the square for both x and y:
   - For x: Add 16 (since \( (-4)^2 = 16 \)) to both sides.
   - For y: Add 9 (since \( (-3)^2 = 9 \)) to both sides.

4. The equation becomes:
   \( (x^2 - 8x + 16) + (y^2 - 6y + 9) = -20 + 16 + 9 \)

5. Simplify the right side:
   \( (x - 4)^2 + (y - 3)^2 = 5 \)

From this, we can see that the center of the circle is at (4, 3), and the radius is \( \sqrt{5} \).

To determine the equation of a circle with its center at \((1, 2)\) and passing through the point \((4, 6)\), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Recall the Standard Equation of a Circle
The standard form of the equation of a circle is:
\[
(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
\]
where:
- \((h, k)\) is the center of the circle.
- \(r\) is the radius.

### Step 2: Plug in the Center Coordinates
Given the center \((1, 2)\), substitute \(h = 1\) and \(k = 2\) into the equation:
\[
(x - 1)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = r^2
\]

### Step 3: Find the Radius Using the Given Point
The circle passes through the point \((4, 6)\). Substitute \(x = 4\) and \(y = 6\) into the equation to find \(r^2\):
\[
(4 - 1)^2 + (6 - 2)^2 = r^2 \\
3^2 + 4^2 = r^2 \\
9 + 16 = r^2 \\
25 = r^2
\]
Therefore, the radius squared (\(r^2\)) is \(25\), and thus:
\[
r = \sqrt{25} = 5
\]

### Step 4: Write the Complete Equation of the Circle
Substitute \(r^2 = 25\) back into the equation from Step 2:
\[
(x - 1)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 25
\]

### Final Answer
The equation of the circle is:
\[
\boxed{(x - 1)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 25}
\]

First, I need to determine the standard equation of an ellipse centered at the origin (0, 0) with a horizontal major axis.

The general form of such an ellipse is x²/a² + y²/b² = 1, where 'a' represents the semi-major axis and 'b' represents the semi-minor axis.

To find the equation of an ellipse with a center at (0, 0), a horizontal major axis of length 10, and a minor axis of length 6, follow these steps:

### Step 1: Understand the Standard Form of an Ellipse

The standard form of an ellipse centered at the origin is:
\[
\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
\]
where:
- \( a \) is the length of the semi-major axis (half the major axis)
- \( b \) is the length of the semi-minor axis (half the minor axis)

### Step 2: Determine the Values of \( a \) and \( b \)

Given:
- Major axis length = 10 ⇒ Semi-major axis (\( a \)) = \( \frac{10}{2} = 5 \)
- Minor axis length = 6 ⇒ Semi-minor axis (\( b \)) = \( \frac{6}{2} = 3 \)

### Step 3: Substitute the Values into the Standard Equation

Substitute \( a = 5 \) and \( b = 3 \) into the standard equation:
\[
\frac{x^2}{5^2} + \frac{y^2}{3^2} = 1
\]
Simplify:
\[
\frac{x^2}{25} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1
\]

### Final Answer

The equation of the ellipse is:

\[
\boxed{\dfrac{x^2}{25}+\dfrac{y^2}{9}=1}
\]

To write the equation of the given ellipse, let's follow these steps:

### **Step 1: Identify the Given Information**

- **Center (h, k):** \( (3, -2) \)
- **Length of Major Axis:** 12 units
- **Length of Minor Axis:** 8 units

Since the major axis is vertical, we'll use the standard form equation for an ellipse with a vertical major axis:

\[
\frac{(x - h)^2}{b^2} + \frac{(y - k)^2}{a^2} = 1
\]

### **Step 2: Determine 'a' and 'b'**

- The semi-major axis (\(a\)) is half the length of the major axis:
  
  \[
  a = \frac{12}{2} = 6
  \]
  
- The semi-minor axis (\(b\)) is half the length of the minor axis:
  
  \[
  b = \frac{8}{2} = 4
  \]

### **Step 3: Substitute 'h', 'k', 'a', and 'b' into the Equation**

Plugging in the known values:

- \( h = 3 \)
- \( k = -2 \)
- \( a^2 = 6^2 = 36 \)
- \( b^2 = 4^2 = 16 \)

\[
\frac{(x - 3)^2}{16} + \frac{(y + 2)^2}{36} = 1

The given equation represents an ellipse with a major axis along the x-axis. Here's how to find its eccentricity:

1. **Identify a² and b²**: From the equation \( \frac{x^2}{36} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1 \), we have \( a^2 = 36 \) (so, \( a = 6 \)) and \( b^2 = 9 \) (so, \( b = 3 \)).

2. **Calculate c**: Using the relationship \( c^2 = a^2 - b^2 \), we find:
   \[
   c = \sqrt{36 - 9} = \sqrt{27} = 3\sqrt{3}
   \]

3. **Find Eccentricity (e)**: The formula is \( e = \frac{c}{a} \). Plugging in the values:
   \[
   e = \frac{3\sqrt{3}}{6} = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}
   \]

The eccentricity of the ellipse is \( \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \).

- **Foci:** The foci are located along the major axis. Given as \((0, 5)\) and \((0, -5)\).

\[
\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

Where:

- \(c\) is the distance from the center to each focus (c=6).

\[
\frac{x^2}{16} - \frac{y^2}{20} = 1

   The given equation is in the standard form of a hyperbola centered at the origin that opens left and right:  
   \[
   \frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
   \]

   For hyperbolas of this form, asymptotes are given by:  
   \[
   y = \pm \frac{b}{a}x
   \]  
   Substituting \( a = 3 \) and \( b = 4 \):  
   \[
   y = \pm \frac{4}{3}x
   \]

   The distance to each focus from the center is \( c \), where:  
   \[
   c^2 = a^2 + b^2
   \]  
   Substituting \( a = 3 \) and \( b = 4 \):  
   \[
   c^2 = 9 + 16 = 25 \quad \Rightarrow \quad c = 5
   \]

   \[
   (\pm5, 0)
   \]

- Asymptotes: \( y = \pm \frac{4}{3}x \)
- Foci: \( (5, 0) \) and \( (-5, 0) \)

Okay, so I need to find the equation of a hyperbola given its foci and vertices. Let me start by recalling what I know about hyperbolas.

First, hyperbolas can be oriented either vertically or horizontally. The orientation depends on whether the foci are aligned along the x-axis or y-axis. In this case, the foci are at (0, ±5) and the vertices are at (0, ±3). Since both coordinates are changing along the y-axis, this hyperbola is oriented vertically.

For a vertical hyperbola, the standard form of the equation is:

(y²/a²) - (x²/b²) = 1

Here, 'a' is the distance from the center to each vertex, and 'c' is the distance from the center to each focus. The relationship between a, b, and c for hyperbolas is given by c² = a² + b².

Given the vertices at (0, ±3), I can determine that 'a' is 3 because it's the distance from the origin (the center) to each vertex.

Similarly, the foci are at (0, ±5), so 'c' is 5 since it's the distance from the center to each focus.

Now, plugging these values into the relationship c² = a² + b²:

5² = 3² + b²  
25 = 9 + b²  
b² = 25 - 9  
b² = 16

So, b is the square root of 16, which is 4. But since we're using it in the equation squared, I don't need to take the square root again.

Now, plugging a² and b² back into the standard form:

(y²/9) - (x²/16) = 1

So that's the equation of the hyperbola.

First off, I remember that a parabola is the set of all points equidistant from a fixed point called the focus and a fixed line called the directrix. Since both the vertex and focus are on the y-axis here (both have x-coordinate 0), this must be a vertical parabola opening upwards.

Now, the standard form for a vertical parabola with vertex at (h, k) is \( y = a(x - h)^2 + k \). In this case, since the vertex is at (0, 0), it simplifies to \( y = ax^2 \).

I also recall that the distance from the vertex to the focus is called 'p'. The formula for the standard form in terms of p is \( y = \frac{1}{4p}x^2 \). Here, since the focus is at (0, 4), and the vertex is at (0, 0), p must be 4. 

Wait, let me make sure I got that right. The distance from the vertex to the focus is indeed 'p', so if the focus is above the vertex, then p is positive, which it should be since 4 is a positive number.

So plugging in p = 4 into the equation \( y = \frac{1}{4p}x^2 \), we get \( y = \frac{1}{16}x^2 \).

Let me double-check that. If I rearrange the standard form, it's usually given as \( (x - h)^2 = 4p(y - k) \). For our case, since h and k are both 0, this simplifies to \( x^2 = 4py \). Solving for y gives \( y = \frac{x^2}{4p} \), which is the same as what I had before. So with p=4, that's \( y = \frac{x^2}{16} \) or \( y = (1/16)x^2 \).

The parabola has its vertex at (0, 0) and focus at (0, 4). Since the focus is above the vertex along the y-axis, the parabola opens upwards. The standard form of such a parabola is \( y = \frac{1}{4p}x^2 \), where p is the distance from the vertex to the focus.

Here, p = 4 (since the focus is at (0, 4)). Plugging this into the equation:

\[ y = \frac{1}{4 \times 4}x^2 = \frac{1}{16}x^2 \]

So, the equation of the parabola is \( y = \frac{x^2}{16} \).

The parabola has vertex (3, 4) and directrix y = 2. Since the directrix is below the vertex, the parabola opens upward.

1. The standard form of a vertical parabola with vertex at (h, k) is:

   \[
   (x - h)^2 = 4p(y - k)
   \]

   Here, \( p \) is the distance from the vertex to the focus and also the distance from the vertex to the directrix.

2. Given:
   - Vertex: (3, 4), so \( h = 3 \) and \( k = 4 \).
   - Directrix: y = 2.

3. Calculate \( p \):
   
   The directrix is located at \( y = k - p \). So:

   \[
   2 = 4 - p \\
   p = 4 - 2 \\
   p = 2
   \]

4. Substitute into the standard form equation:
   
   \[
   (x - 3)^2 = 4(2)(y - 4) \\
   (x - 3)^2 = 8(y - 4)
   \]

5. Expanding this gives:

   \[
   x^2 - 6x + 9 = 8y - 32 \\
   x^2 - 6x + 41 = 8y
   \]
   
   Solving for \( y \):

   \[
   y = \frac{x^2}{8} - \frac{6x}{8} + \frac{41}{8}
   \]

**Final Answer:**

The equation of the parabola is \((x - 3)^2 = 8(y - 4)\) or in slope-intercept form, \(y = \frac{x^2 - 6x + 41}{8}\).

**Final Answer**
The equation of the parabola is \boxed{(x + \frac{3}{4})^2 = -4k(y - k)}.
</think>

Given that the vertex of the parabola is at \((h, k)\) and it passes through a point with \(x\)-coordinate \(-\frac{3}{4}\), we need to determine the equation of the parabola.

1. Recognize that the problem involves a vertical parabola because the focus must be aligned vertically with the vertex.
2. For a vertical parabola, the standard form is \((x - h)^2 = 4p(y - k)\), where \((h, k + p)\) is the focus.
3. Given that the focus is at \(\left(-\frac{3}{4}, 0\right)\), we know the vertex must be directly above or below this point on the same \(x\)-coordinate. Therefore, \(h = -\frac{3}{4}\).
4. The distance from the vertex to the focus (which is \(|p|\)) is equal to \(|k + p|\). Since the focus is at \(\left(-\frac{3}{4}, 0\right)\), we have:
   \[
   k + p = 0 \implies p = -k
   \]
5. Substitute \(h = -\frac{3}{4}\) and \(p = -k\) into the standard form of the vertical parabola:
   \[
   (x - (-\\frac{3}{4}))^2 = 4(-k)(y - k)
   \]
6. Simplify this to get the final equation:
   \[
   (x + \frac{3}{4})^2 = -4k(y - k)

The given standard form equation is \( x^2 - 4x + 8y + 12 = 0 \). To convert this into vertex form:

1. Rearrange to solve for y:
   \[
   x^2 - 4x + 8y + 12 = 0 \\
   x^2 - 4x = -8y - 12
   \]
   
2. Complete the square for \( x^2 - 4x \):
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 = -8y - 8
   \]
   
3. Solve for y:
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 = -8(y + 1) \\
   y = -\frac{1}{8}(x - 2)^2 - 1
   \]

The vertex form is \( y = -\frac{1}{8}(x - 2)^2 - 1 \).

To find the equation of the parabola with the given vertex and directrix, follow these steps:

1. **Identify the Vertex and Directrix**:
   - Vertex: (2, -1)
   - Directrix: y = -4

2. **Determine the Direction of the Parabola**:
   Since the directrix is a horizontal line below the vertex, the parabola opens **upwards**.

3. **Calculate the Distance Between the Vertex and the Focus (a)**:
   The distance between the vertex and the focus (denoted as \( a \)) can be found using the formula for the directrix in a vertical parabola:
   
   \[
   \text{Directrix} = y = k - \frac{1}{4a}
   \]
   
   Plugging in the known values:
   
   \[
   -4 = -1 - \frac{1}{4a}
   \]
   
   Solving for \( a \):
   
   \[
   -3 = -\frac{1}{4a} \\
   4a = \frac{1}{3} \\
   a = \frac{1}{12}
   \]

4. **Write the Equation of the Parabola**:
   The standard form of a vertical parabola's equation is:
   
   \[
   (x - h)^2 = 4a(y - k)
   \]
   
   Plugging in \( h = 2 \), \( k = -1 \), and \( a = \frac{1}{12} \):
   
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 = 4\left(\frac{1}{12}\right)(y + 1)
   \]
   
   Simplify:
   
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 = \frac{1}{3}(y + 1)
   \]

To determine the equation of a circle given the endpoints of its diameter, I first identify the coordinates of these points. The endpoints are (-2, 1) and (6, -3).

Next, I calculate the center of the circle by finding the midpoint between these two points. The midpoint formula gives me an average of the x-coordinates and an average of the y-coordinates.

After determining the center, I find the radius by calculating half the distance between the two endpoints using the distance formula.

Finally, with the center (h, k) and radius r known, I can write the standard equation of the circle.

To determine the equation of a circle given the endpoints of its diameter at \((-2, 1)\) and \((6, -3)\), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Find the Center of the Circle
The center \((h, k)\) is the midpoint between the two endpoints. The midpoint formula is:
\[
h = \frac{x_1 + x_2}{2}, \quad k = \frac{y_1 + y_2}{2}
\]
Plugging in the given points:
\[
h = \frac{-2 + 6}{2} = \frac{4}{2} = 2
\]
\[
k = \frac{1 + (-3)}{2} = \frac{-2}{2} = -1
\]
So, the center is \((2, -1)\).

### Step 2: Calculate the Radius of the Circle
The radius \(r\) is half the distance between the two endpoints. First, find the distance using the distance formula:
\[
\text{Distance} = \sqrt{(x_2 - x_1)^2 + (y_2 - y_1)^2}
\]
Plugging in the points:
\[
\text{Distance} = \sqrt{(6 - (-2))^2 + (-3 - 1)^2} = \sqrt{8^2 + (-4)^2} = \sqrt{64 + 16} = \sqrt{80}
\]
Simplify \(\sqrt{80}\):
\[
\sqrt{80} = \sqrt{16 \times 5} = 4\sqrt{5}
\]
So, the radius \(r\) is half of this distance:
\[
r = \frac{4\sqrt{5}}{2} = 2\sqrt{5}
\]

### Step 3: Write the Equation of the Circle
The standard form of a circle's equation with center \((h, k)\) and radius \(r\) is:
\[
(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
\]
Substitute \(h = 2\), \(k = -1\), and \(r = 2\sqrt{5}\):
\[
(x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = (2\sqrt{5})^2
\]
Simplify the right side:
\[
(2\sqrt{5})^2 = 4 \times 5 = 20
\]

### Final Answer
The equation of the circle is:
\[
\boxed{(x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 20}
\]

\[ x^2 + y^2 + 6x - 4y - 12 = 0 \]

we can rewrite it in the standard form of a circle's equation, which is:

\[ (x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2 \]

where \((h, k)\) is the center and \(r\) is the radius.

### Step-by-Step Solution:

   \[ x^2 + 6x + y^2 - 4y = 12 \]

2. **Complete the square for the \(x\)-terms and \(y\)-terms:**

   - For \(x^2 + 6x\):
     \[
     x^2 + 6x = (x + 3)^2 - 9
     \]
   
   - For \(y^2 - 4y\):
     \[
     y^2 - 4y = (y - 2)^2 - 4
     \]

3. **Substitute the completed squares back into the equation:**

   \[

   \]

4. **Simplify the equation:**

   \[
   (x + 3)^2 + (y - 2)^2 - 13 = 12 \\
   (x + 3)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 25
   \]

   - The radius \( r \) is the square root of the constant term on the right side:
     \[
     r = \sqrt{25} = 5
     \]

### Final Answer:

\[
\boxed{\text{Center: } (-3,\ 2),\ \text{Radius: } 5}
\]

1. **Start with the given equation:**
   \[
   x^2 + y^2 - 4x + 2y - 11 = 0
   \]

2. **Rearrange and group terms involving \(x\) and \(y\):**
   \[
   (x^2 - 4x) + (y^2 + 2y) = 11
   \]

3. **Complete the square for the \(x\)-terms:**
   - Take half of the coefficient of \(x\), which is \(-2\).
   - Square it to get \(4\).
   \[
   x^2 - 4x + 4 = (x - 2)^2
   \]

4. **Complete the square for the \(y\)-terms:**
   - Take half of the coefficient of \(y\), which is \(1\).
   - Square it to get \(1\).
   \[
   y^2 + 2y + 1 = (y + 1)^2
   \]

5. **Add these squares to both sides of the equation:**
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 4 + 1 + 11
   \]
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 16
   \]

6. **Write the standard form of the circle:**
   The equation is now in the standard form of a circle with center \((h, k) = (2, -1)\) and radius \(r = 4\).

The standard form of the given circle is:

To find the equation of a circle with its center at \((-2, 5)\) that passes through the point \((1, 9)\), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Recall the Standard Equation of a Circle
The standard form of the equation of a circle is:
\[
(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
\]
where:
- \((h, k)\) are the coordinates of the center.
- \(r\) is the radius.

### Step 2: Identify the Center and Radius
From the problem:
- The center of the circle is \((-2, 5)\), so \(h = -2\) and \(k = 5\).
- To find the radius \(r\), use the distance formula between the center and the given point \((1, 9)\).

### Step 3: Calculate the Radius
Using the distance formula:
\[
r = \sqrt{(x_2 - x_1)^2 + (y_2 - y_1)^2}
\]
Substitute the coordinates:
\[
r = \sqrt{(1 - (-2))^2 + (9 - 5)^2} = \sqrt{(3)^2 + (4)^2} = \sqrt{9 + 16} = \sqrt{25} = 5
\]

### Step 4: Substitute into the Standard Equation
Now, plug \(h = -2\), \(k = 5\), and \(r = 5\) into the standard equation:
\[
(x - (-2))^2 + (y - 5)^2 = 5^2
\]
Simplify:
\[
(x + 2)^2 + (y - 5)^2 = 25
\]

### Final Answer: 
The equation of the circle is:

\( y^2 - 6y \) can be written as \( (y - 3)^2 - 9 \).

To find the radius and center of the circle given by the equation \( x^2 + y^2 - 8x - 6y + 20 = 0 \), we can rewrite it in the standard form of a circle's equation, which is:

\[
(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
\]

where \((h, k)\) is the center and \(r\) is the radius.

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **Start with the given equation:**
   \[
   x^2 + y^2 - 8x - 6y + 20 = 0
   \]

2. **Rearrange the terms to group \(x\)-terms and \(y\)-terms together:**
   \[
   (x^2 - 8x) + (y^2 - 6y) + 20 = 0
   \]

3. **Complete the square for the \(x\)-terms:**
   - Take the coefficient of \(x\), which is \(-8\), divide by 2 to get \(-4\), and then square it to get \(16\).
   - Add and subtract \(16\) within the equation:
     \[
     (x^2 - 8x + 16 - 16) = (x - 4)^2 - 16
     \]

4. **Complete the square for the \(y\)-terms:**
   - Take the coefficient of \(y\), which is \(-6\), divide by 2 to get \(-3\), and then square it to get \(9\).
   - Add and subtract \(9\) within the equation:
     \[
     (y^2 - 6y + 9 - 9) = (y - 3)^2 - 9
     \]

5. **Substitute the completed squares back into the original equation:**
   \[
   (x - 4)^2 - 16 + (y - 3)^2 - 9 + 20 = 0
   \]

6. **Combine the constant terms:**
   \[
   (x - 4)^2 + (y - 3)^2 - 5 = 0
   \]
   \[
   (x - 4)^2 + (y - 3)^2 = 5
   \]

7. **Identify the center and radius from the standard form:**
   - The center \((h, k)\) is \( (4, 3) \).
   - The radius \( r \) is \( \sqrt{5} \).

### Final Answer:
\[
\boxed{\text{Center: }(4,\ 3),\ \text{Radius: }\sqrt{5}}
\]

To determine the equation of a circle with its center at (1, 2) and passing through the point (4, 6), we'll follow these steps:

**Step 1: Recall the General Equation of a Circle**

The standard form of the equation of a circle is:
\[
(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
\]
where \((h, k)\) is the center and \(r\) is the radius.

**Step 2: Identify the Center Coordinates**

From the problem, the center of the circle is at (1, 2). Therefore:
\[
h = 1,\quad k = 2
\]

**Step 3: Plug the Center into the Equation**

Substituting \(h\) and \(k\) into the general equation:
\[
(x - 1)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = r^2
\]

**Step 4: Find the Radius Using the Given Point**

The circle passes through the point (4, 6). This means when \(x = 4\) and \(y = 6\), the equation should hold true.

Substitute these values into the equation:
\[
(4 - 1)^2 + (6 - 2)^2 = r^2
\]
\[
3^2 + 4^2 = r^2
\]
\[
9 + 16 = r^2
\]
\[
r^2 = 25
\]
\[
r = \sqrt{25} = 5
\]

Now that we know \(r^2 = 25\), substitute it back into the equation:
\[
(x - 1)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 25
\]

The problem involves finding the equation of an ellipse given its center, major axis, and minor axis lengths.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify Key Information:**
   - Center at (0, 0): This means there are no shifts in the standard form of the ellipse equation.
   - Horizontal major axis with length 10: The semi-major axis (a) is half of this, so a = 10 / 2 = 5. Since it's horizontal, a² will be under the x-term.

2. **Determine Semi-Major Axis (a):**
   - Major axis length = 10 → semi-major axis (a) = 5.
   - Therefore, a² = 5² = 25.

3. **Find Semi-Minor Axis (b):**
   - Minor axis length = 6 → semi-minor axis (b) = 6 / 2 = 3.
   - Thus, b² = 3² = 9.

4. **Formulate the Ellipse Equation:**
   - Since the major axis is horizontal, the standard form of the ellipse equation is:
     \[
     \frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
     \]
   - Plugging in a² and b²:
     \[
     \frac{x^2}{25} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1
     \]

**Final Answer:**
The equation of the ellipse is \(\frac{x^2}{25} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1\).

To find the equation of an ellipse with a center at (3, -2), a vertical major axis of length 12, and a minor axis of length 8, follow these steps:

### Step 1: Understand the Standard Form of an Ellipse
The standard form of an ellipse centered at \((h, k)\) is:
\[
\frac{(x - h)^2}{b^2} + \frac{(y - k)^2}{a^2} = 1
\]
where:
- \(2a\) is the length of the major axis.
- \(2b\) is the length of the minor axis.

Since the major axis is **vertical**, the equation uses \(a^2\) under the \((y - k)\) term and \(b^2\) under the \((x - h)\) term.

### Step 2: Determine \(a\) and \(b\)
Given:
- Length of the major axis (\(2a\)) = 12 ⇒ \(a = \frac{12}{2} = 6\)
- Length of the minor axis (\(2b\)) = 8 ⇒ \(b = \frac{8}{2} = 4\)

### Step 3: Plug in the Values
The center is (3, -2), so \(h = 3\) and \(k = -2\).

Substitute these into the standard form:
\[
\frac{(x - 3)^2}{4^2} + \frac{(y - (-2))^2}{6^2} = 1
\]
Simplify:
\[
\frac{(x - 3)^2}{16} + \frac{(y + 2)^2}{36} = 1
\]

### Final Answer: 

The equation of the ellipse is:

\[
\boxed{\frac{(x-3)^{2}}{16}+\frac{(y+2)^{2}}{36}=1}
\]

   \]

   \[
   \frac{x^2}{4} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1

Okay, so I need to find the eccentricity of the ellipse given by the equation x²/36 + y²/9 = 1. Hmm, let me think about how ellipses work.

First, I remember that the standard form of an ellipse is either (x-h)²/a² + (y-k)²/b² = 1 or vice versa, where (h,k) is the center. In this case, the equation is x²/36 + y²/9 = 1, which looks like it's centered at the origin because there are no h or k terms.

Now, I need to figure out whether it's a horizontal or vertical ellipse. The denominators under x² and y² tell me that if the larger denominator is under x², it's a horizontal ellipse, meaning it's wider along the x-axis. If it's under y², then it's taller vertically.

In this equation, 36 is bigger than 9, so since 36 is under x², the major axis is along the x-axis. That makes sense because 'a' is always associated with the major axis length, and 'b' with the minor.

So, for a horizontal ellipse, I know that the standard form is (x²/a²) + (y²/b²) = 1. Comparing this to my equation, a² is 36, so a must be sqrt(36) which is 6. Similarly, b² is 9, so b is sqrt(9) which is 3.

Now, I need the formula for eccentricity of an ellipse. From what I recall, the eccentricity (e) measures how "stretched" the ellipse is. The formula is e = c/a, where c is the distance from the center to a focus.

But wait, how do I find c? Oh right! There's another relationship in ellipses: c² = a² - b². That makes sense because if it were equal, you'd have a circle, and when a increases beyond b, c becomes larger, indicating more eccentricity.

So plugging in the values I have:

c² = a² - b²
c² = 36 - 9
c² = 27

Taking the square root of both sides, c is sqrt(27). Simplifying that, sqrt(27) is 3*sqrt(3), but maybe it's better to leave it as sqrt(27) for now.

Now, going back to eccentricity e = c/a. Substituting in:

e = sqrt(27)/6

But I can simplify sqrt(27). Since 27 is 9*3, so sqrt(27) = 3*sqrt(3). Therefore,

e = (3*sqrt(3))/6

Simplifying the fraction by dividing numerator and denominator by 3:

e = sqrt(3)/2

Wait, that seems right. So the eccentricity is sqrt(3)/2.

Let me just double-check my steps to make sure I didn't make a mistake. Started with standard form, identified a²=36 and b²=9, so a=6, b=3. Then used c² = a² - b² which gave 27, then e=c/a is sqrt(27)/6 simplifying to sqrt(3)/2. Yep, that all looks correct.

I think I'm confident with this answer now.

The equation of the ellipse given is in standard form with \(a^2 = 36\) and \(b^2 = 9\). Calculating the eccentricity involves finding \(c\) using \(c^2 = a^2 - b^2\), which gives \(c = \sqrt{27}\). The eccentricity \(e = \frac{c}{a} = \frac{\sqrt{27}}{6} = \frac{3\sqrt{3}}{6} = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\).

To find the equation of an ellipse that passes through the points (3, 0) and (0, 2) with a horizontal major axis, we'll follow these steps:

### Step 1: Standard Form of the Ellipse
The standard form of an ellipse with a horizontal major axis is:
\[
\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
\]
where \( a \) is the semi-major axis and \( b \) is the semi-minor axis.

### Step 2: Substitute the Given Points

**Point (3, 0):**
\[
\frac{3^2}{a^2} + \frac{0^2}{b^2} = 1 \\
\Rightarrow \frac{9}{a^2} = 1 \\
\Rightarrow a^2 = 9 \\
\Rightarrow a = 3
\]

**Point (0, 2):**
\[
\frac{0^2}{a^2} + \frac{2^2}{b^2} = 1 \\
\Rightarrow \frac{4}{b^2} = 1 \\
\Rightarrow b^2 = 4 \\
\Rightarrow b = 2
\]

### Step 3: Write the Equation of the Ellipse
Substituting \( a = 3 \) and \( b = 2 \) into the standard form:
\[
\frac{x^2}{9} + \frac{y^2}{4} = 1
\]

\]
where:
- \( (h, k) \) is the center of the ellipse.
- \( a \) is the semi-major axis length (\( a > b \)).
- \( b \) is the semi-minor axis length.

The hyperbola given by the equation \( \frac{x^2}{9} - \frac{y^2}{16} = 1 \) has a horizontal transverse axis. 

- **Asymptotes**: The equations are found using \( y = \pm \frac{b}{a}x \), where \( a^2 = 9 \) (so \( a = 3 \)) and \( b^2 = 16 \) (so \( b = 4 \)). Thus, the asymptotes are \( y = \pm \frac{4}{3}x \).

- **Foci**: The distance from the center to each focus is calculated using \( c \), where \( c^2 = a^2 + b^2 \). Substituting the values, \( c^2 = 9 + 16 = 25 \), so \( c = 5 \). Since the hyperbola opens horizontally, the foci are at \( (5, 0) \) and \( (-5, 0) \).

**Final Answer:**
The asymptotes of the hyperbola are \( y = \frac{4}{3}x \) and \( y = -\frac{4}{3}x \), and the foci are at (5, 0) and (-5, 0).

The problem involves determining the equation of a hyperbola with given parameters but missing orientation (horizontal or vertical). Here's how to approach it step by step:

1. **Identify Parameters:**
   - Center at (-3, 4)
   - Transverse axis length = 10 ⇒ \( a = \frac{10}{2} = 5 \)
   - Conjugate axis length = 8 ⇒ \( b = \frac{8}{2} = 4 \)

2. **Determine Possible Equations:**
   Since the orientation (horizontal or vertical) isn't specified, both possibilities need to be considered.

3. **Case 1: Horizontal Transverse Axis**
   - The standard form is:
     \[
     \frac{(x + 3)^2}{5^2} - \frac{(y - 4)^2}{4^2} = 1
     \]
     Simplifying,
     \[
     \frac{(x + 3)^2}{25} - \frac{(y - 4)^2}{16} = 1
     \]

   **Case 2: Vertical Transverse Axis**
   - The standard form is:
     \[
     \frac{(y - 4)^2}{5^2} - \frac{(x + 3)^2}{4^2} = 1
     \]
     Simplifying,
     \[
     \frac{(y - 4)^2}{25} - \frac{(x + 3)^2}{16} = 1
     \]

**Final Answer:**
There are two possible equations for the hyperbola, depending on its orientation.

- If it opens horizontally:
\[
\boxed{\dfrac{(x + 3)^2}{25} - \dfrac{(y - 4)^2}{16} = 1}
\]

- If it opens vertically:
\[
\boxed{\dfrac{(y - 4)^2}{25} - \dfrac{(x + 3)^2}{16} = 1}
\]

The given equation \(4x^2 - 9y^2 = 36\) can be converted into the standard form of a hyperbola by dividing both sides by 36. This results in \(\frac{x^2}{9} - \frac{y^2}{4} = 1\).

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the type of conic section:** The equation has \(x^2\) and \(y^2\) terms with opposite signs, indicating a hyperbola.

2. **Determine the standard form for a hyperbola opening horizontally:** The standard form is \(\frac{(x-h)^2}{a^2} - \frac{(y-k)^2}{b^2} = 1\), where \(h\) and \(k\) are the coordinates of the center.

3. **Divide each term by 36 to normalize the equation:**
   \[
   \frac{4x^2}{36} - \frac{9y^2}{36} = \frac{36}{36}
   \]
   
4. **Simplify each fraction:**
   - \( \frac{4}{36} = \frac{1}{9} \) so the first term becomes \( \frac{x^2}{9} \).
   - \( \frac{9}{36} = \frac{1}{4} \) so the second term becomes \( \frac{y^2}{4} \).
   - The right side simplifies to 1.

5. **Write the equation in standard form:**
   \[
   \frac{x^2}{9} - \frac{y^2}{4} = 1
   \]

**Final Answer:** {Standard Form: "x squared over 9 minus y squared over 4 equals 1"}

I recall that for hyperbolas, the relationship between a, b, and c (which is the distance from the center to each focus) is given by \( c^2 = a^2 + b^2 \). Once I find c, I can compute the eccentricity as \( e = \frac{c}{a} \).

Now compute e as \( e = \frac{c}{a} = \frac{\sqrt{41}}{5} \). Let me just double-check that: a=5, b=4, so c² is definitely 25 + 16 which is 41. Yep.

The hyperbola has its center at the origin since both vertices and foci are symmetric about it. Given the vertices at (0, ±3), we determine that a = 3. The foci at (0, ±5) indicate c = 5. Using the relationship \( c^2 = a^2 + b^2 \), we find:

\( 5^2 = 3^2 + b^2 \)

Solving for \( b^2 \):

\( 25 = 9 + b^2 \)
\( b^2 = 16 \)

\(\frac{y^2}{9} - \frac{x^2}{16} = 1\)

To determine the equation of the hyperbola with vertices at (5, 0) and (-5, 0) and asymptotes with slopes of ±3/5, follow these steps:

### Step 1: Identify Key Information
- **Vertices:** The vertices are located at (5, 0) and (-5, 0). This means:
  - The hyperbola is centered at the midpoint between the vertices.
  - The distance from the center to each vertex is \( a \).

### Step 2: Determine the Center and Value of \( a \)
- **Center (\( h, k \)):** Since the vertices are symmetric about the origin (0, 0), the center of the hyperbola is at (0, 0).
  
- **Value of \( a \):** The distance from the center to each vertex is:
  \[
  a = 5
  \]

### Step 3: Calculate \( b \) Using Asymptote Slopes
The slopes of the asymptotes for a hyperbola centered at (0, 0) are given by:
\[
\text{Slope} = \pm \frac{b}{a}
\]
Given that the slopes are ±3/5, we set up the equation:
\[
\frac{b}{a} = \frac{3}{5}
\]
Substitute \( a = 5 \) into this equation to find \( b \):
\[
\frac{b}{5} = \frac{3}{5} \\
b = 3
\]

### Step 4: Write the Equation of the Hyperbola
The standard form of a hyperbola centered at (0, 0) opening horizontally is:
\[
\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
\]
Substitute \( a = 5 \) and \( b = 3 \):
\[
\frac{x^2}{5^2} - \frac{y^2}{3^2} = 1 \\
\frac{x^2}{25} - \frac{y^2}{9} = 1
\]

### Final Answer:  
\boxed{\dfrac{x^2}{25} - \dfrac{y^2}{9} = 1}

To find the equation of a parabola with the given focus at \((2, 3)\) and directrix \(x = -1\), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Determine the Direction and Vertex
Since the directrix is a vertical line (\(x = -1\)), the parabola opens horizontally.

The vertex of the parabola lies exactly halfway between the focus \((2, 3)\) and the directrix \(x = -1\).

- **Calculate the x-coordinate of the vertex:**
  \[
  h = \frac{2 + (-1)}{2} = \frac{1}{2} = 0.5
  \]
  
- **The y-coordinate of the vertex** remains the same as that of the focus:
  \[
  k = 3
  \]
  
Thus, the vertex is at \((h, k) = (0.5, 3)\).

### Step 2: Calculate the Parameter \(p\)
\(p\) is the distance from the vertex to the focus.

- **Difference in x-coordinates:**
  \[
  p = 2 - 0.5 = 1.5
  \]

### Step 3: Write the Equation of the Parabola
For a horizontally opening parabola with vertex at \((h, k)\) and parameter \(p\), the standard form is:
\[
(y - k)^2 = 4p(x - h)
\]
Substitute \(h = 0.5\), \(k = 3\), and \(p = 1.5\) into the equation:

\[
(y - 3)^2 = 4 \times 1.5 \times (x - 0.5) \\
(y - 3)^2 = 6(x - 0.5)
\]

### Final Answer
The equation of the parabola is:
\[
\boxed{(y - 3)^2 = 6(x - 0.5)}
\]

The given quadratic equation \( y = 3x^2 + 6x + 5 \) can be converted to vertex form by completing the square.

1. Factor out the coefficient of \( x^2 \):
   \[
   y = 3(x^2 + 2x) + 5
   \]
   
2. Complete the square inside the parentheses:
   - Take half of the coefficient of \( x \): \( \frac{2}{2} = 1 \)
   - Square it: \( 1^2 = 1 \)
   - Add and subtract this value inside the parentheses, remembering to balance the equation by adding an equivalent amount outside.

3. Adjust for the added term:
   \[
   y = 3(x^2 + 2x + 1) - 3(1) + 5
   \]
   
4. Simplify:
   \[
   y = 3(x + 1)^2 + ( -3 + 5 ) \\
   y = 3(x + 1)^2 + 2
   \]

The vertex form of the parabola is \( y = 3(x + 1)^2 + 2 \).

Final Answer:
There is no such parabola as it results in a degenerate form.

Step-by-step explanation:

1. **Problem Setup**: The problem states that a parabola has its focus at (0, 3) and directrix y = 3.
2. **Analysis of Focus and Directrix Position**:
   - Both the focus and the directrix are located on the horizontal line y = 3.
   - For a standard parabola with vertical orientation, the focus lies above or below the vertex, and the directrix is a horizontal line at a distance p from the vertex. Here, both coincide exactly on y = 3.
3. **Equation Derivation**:
   - The definition of a parabola states that any point (x, y) on the parabola must be equidistant from the focus and directrix.
   - Distance from (x, y) to focus (0, 3): √(x² + (y - 3)²)
   - Perpendicular distance from (x, y) to directrix y = 3: |y - 3|
   - Setting these equal: √(x² + (y - 3)²) = |y - 3|
4. **Simplifying the Equation**:
   - Squaring both sides: x² + (y - 3)² = (y - 3)²
   - Simplifies to x² = 0, hence x = 0.
5. **Conclusion**:
   - The resulting equation is a vertical line at x = 0, which indicates that the set of points satisfying these conditions forms a degenerate parabola—essentially a straight line—and not a proper parabola as it does not have a curvature.

Answer: There is no such parabola.

- From (2,8): 2a + b = 2
- From (-1,3): a - b = -1

First, I need to determine the center of the circle by finding the midpoint of the given endpoints of the diameter. The endpoints are (-2, 1) and (6, -3). 

To find the midpoint \( M \), I'll use the midpoint formula:
\[ M = \left( \frac{x_1 + x_2}{2}, \frac{y_1 + y_2}{2} \right) \]
Plugging in the values:
\[
x_{mid} = \frac{-2 + 6}{2} = 2
\]
\[
y_{mid} = \frac{1 + (-3)}{2} = -1
\]
So, the center of the circle is (2, -1).

Next, I'll calculate the radius by finding half the distance between the two endpoints. The distance formula is:
\[ d = \sqrt{(x_2 - x_1)^2 + (y_2 - y_1)^2} \]
Substituting the given points:
\[
d = \sqrt{(6 - (-2))^2 + (-3 - 1)^2} = \sqrt{8^2 + (-4)^2} = \sqrt{64 + 16} = \sqrt{80}
\]
The radius \( r \) is half of this distance:
\[ r = \frac{\sqrt{80}}{2} = \sqrt{20} \]

Finally, I'll write the equation of the circle using the standard form:
\[ (x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2 \]
Substituting \( h = 2 \), \( k = -1 \), and \( r = \sqrt{20} \):
\[
(x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = (\sqrt{20})^2
\]
Simplifying:
\[
(x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 20
\]

**Solution:**

To determine the equation of a circle with endpoints of its diameter at \((-2, 1)\) and \((6, -3)\), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Find the Center of the Circle
The center \((h, k)\) is the midpoint of the diameter. Use the midpoint formula:
\[
h = \frac{-2 + 6}{2} = \frac{4}{2} = 2
\]
\[
k = \frac{1 + (-3)}{2} = \frac{-2}{2} = -1
\]
So, the center of the circle is:
\[
(h, k) = (2, -1)
\]

### Step 2: Calculate the Radius
The radius \(r\) is half the distance between the two endpoints. First, find the distance using the distance formula:
\[
d = \sqrt{(6 - (-2))^2 + (-3 - 1)^2} = \sqrt{8^2 + (-4)^2} = \sqrt{64 + 16} = \sqrt{80}
\]
Then, calculate the radius:
\[
r = \frac{\sqrt{80}}{2} = \sqrt{20}
\]

### Step 3: Write the Equation of the Circle
The standard form of a circle's equation is:
\[
(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
\]
Substituting \(h = 2\), \(k = -1\), and \(r = \sqrt{20}\):
\[
(x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = (\sqrt{20})^2 \\
(x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 20
\]

The given equation of the circle is \( x^2 + y^2 + 6x - 4y - 12 = 0 \). 

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Group the terms:**  
   Combine the \( x \) terms and \( y \) terms together:
   \[
   (x^2 + 6x) + (y^2 - 4y) = 12
   \]

2. **Complete the square for \( x \):**  
   Take half of the coefficient of \( x \), which is \( 3 \), and square it to get \( 9 \). Add and subtract this inside the equation:
   \[
   (x^2 + 6x + 9 - 9) 
   \]
   This becomes:
   \[
   (x + 3)^2 - 9
   \]

3. **Complete the square for \( y \):**  
   Take half of the coefficient of \( y \), which is \( -2 \), and square it to get \( 4 \). Add and subtract this inside the equation:
   \[
   (y^2 - 4y + 4 - 4) 
   \]
   This becomes:
   \[
   (y - 2)^2 - 4
   \]

4. **Combine the completed squares:**  
   Substitute back into the original equation and balance both sides by adding \( 9 + 4 = 13 \) to the right side:
   \[
   (x + 3)^2 - 9 + (y - 2)^2 - 4 = 12
   \]
   Simplify:
   \[
   (x + 3)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 12 + 9 + 4 
   \]
   \[
   (x + 3)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 25
   \]

5. **Identify the center and radius:**  
   Comparing with the standard form \( (x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2 \):
   - Center (\( h, k \)) is \( (-3, 2) \)
   - Radius (\( r \)) is \( \sqrt{25} = 5 \)

**Final Answer:**  
Center: (-3, 2), Radius: 5

To find the equation of the circle with its center at \((-2, 5)\) and passing through the point \((1, 9)\), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Identify the Center
The center of the circle is given as:
\[
(h, k) = (-2, 5)
\]

### Step 2: Calculate the Radius
The radius \(r\) can be found using the distance formula between the center and the point \((1, 9)\):
\[
r = \sqrt{(x_2 - x_1)^2 + (y_2 - y_1)^2}
\]
Substitute the coordinates:
\[
r = \sqrt{(1 - (-2))^2 + (9 - 5)^2} = \sqrt{3^2 + 4^2} = \sqrt{9 + 16} = \sqrt{25} = 5
\]

### Step 3: Write the Equation of the Circle
The standard equation of a circle is:
\[
(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
\]
Substitute \(h = -2\), \(k = 5\), and \(r = 5\):
\[
(x - (-2))^2 + (y - 5)^2 = 5^2
\]
Simplify:
\[
(x + 2)^2 + (y - 5)^2 = 25
\]

### Final Answer: \(\boxed{(x+2)^{2}+(y-5)^{2}=25}\)

To determine the points of intersection between the line \( y = x + 3 \) and the circle \( x^2 + y^2 = 17 \), follow these steps:

### **Step 1: Substitute the Equation of the Line into the Circle's Equation**

Since \( y = x + 3 \), substitute this expression for \( y \) in the circle's equation:
\[
x^2 + (x + 3)^2 = 17
\]

### **Step 2: Expand and Simplify the Equation**

First, expand \( (x + 3)^2 \):
\[
(x + 3)^2 = x^2 + 6x + 9
\]
Now, substitute back into the equation:
\[
x^2 + x^2 + 6x + 9 = 17 \\
2x^2 + 6x + 9 = 17
\]
Subtract 17 from both sides to set the equation to zero:
\[
2x^2 + 6x - 8 = 0
\]

### **Step 3: Solve for \( x \) Using the Quadratic Formula**

The quadratic equation is of the form \( ax^2 + bx + c = 0 \), where:
- \( a = 2 \)
- \( b = 6 \)
- \( c = -8 \)

Apply the quadratic formula:
\[
x = \frac{-b \pm \sqrt{b^2 - 4ac}}{2a}
\]
Plugging in the values:
\[
x = \frac{-6 \pm \sqrt{(6)^2 - 4(2)(-8)}}{2(2)} \\
x = \frac{-6 \pm \sqrt{36 + 64}}{4} \\
x = \frac{-6 \pm \sqrt{100}}{4} \\
x = \frac{-6 \pm 10}{4}
\]

This gives two solutions:
\[
x_1 = \frac{-6 + 10}{4} = \frac{4}{4} = 1 \\
x_2 = \frac{-6 - 10}{4} = \frac{-16}{4} = -4
\]

### **Step 4: Find the Corresponding \( y \)-Coordinates**

Using \( y = x + 3 \):

For \( x = 1 \):
\[
y = 1 + 3 = 4
\]
So, one point of intersection is \( (1, 4) \).

For \( x = -4 \):
\[
y = -4 + 3 = -1
\]
The other point of intersection is \( (-4, -1) \).

### **Final Answer**

The points where the line intersects the circle are:

**{Final Answer: "points_of_intersection": [ {x: 1, y: 4}, {x: -4, y: -1} ] }**

Similarly, the minor axis length is 6, so 'b' would be half of that, which is b = 6/2 = 3.

Since the major axis is horizontal, the standard form of the ellipse equation remains (x²/a²) + (y²/b²) = 1 without any modifications.

Substituting the values of 'a' and 'b', I substitute a² with 5² = 25 and b² with 3² = 9 into the equation. This results in x²/25 + y²/9 = 1.

To find the equation of an ellipse centered at (0, 0) with a horizontal major axis of length 10 and a minor axis of length 6:

1. **Determine the semi-major and semi-minor axes:**
   - Major axis length is 10, so semi-major axis \( a = \frac{10}{2} = 5 \).
   - Minor axis length is 6, so semi-minor axis \( b = \frac{6}{2} = 3 \).

2. **Write the standard equation of an ellipse:**
   
   Since the major axis is horizontal and the center is at (0, 0), the standard form is:
   
   \[
   \frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
   \]
   
3. **Substitute \( a \) and \( b \):**
   
   Plugging in \( a = 5 \) and \( b = 3 \):
   
   \[
   \frac{x^2}{5^2} + \frac{y^2}{3^2} = 1
   \]
   
4. **Simplify the equation:**
   
   \[
   \frac{x^2}{25} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
The equation of the ellipse is \(\boxed{\dfrac{x^{2}}{25}+\dfrac{y^{2}}{9}=1}\).

I need to find the equation of an ellipse given its center, lengths of axes, and orientation.

First, identify the standard form of the equation for an ellipse centered at (h, k) with a vertical major axis. Since it's vertical, the y-term will be positive in the equation.

Next, determine the values of a and b. The major axis length is 12, so half of that gives a = 6. The minor axis length is 8, so half of that gives b = 4.

</think>

To write the equation of an ellipse with the given specifications:

**Given:**
- **Center (h, k):** (3, -2)
- **Length of major axis:** 12
- **Length of minor axis:** 8
- **Major axis is vertical**

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Determine the standard form of an ellipse equation with a vertical major axis:**
   
   The general formula for such an ellipse centered at (h, k) is:
   
   \[
   \frac{(x - h)^2}{b^2} + \frac{(y - k)^2}{a^2} = 1
   \]
   
   Here, \( a > b \), with \( a \) being the semi-major axis and \( b \) the semi-minor axis.

2. **Calculate the lengths of the semi-major and semi-minor axes:**
   
   - **Semi-major axis (a):** 
     
     Since the major axis is vertical and has a length of 12, the semi-major axis length is:
     
     \[
     a = \frac{12}{2} = 6
     \]
   
   - **Semi-minor axis (b):** 
     
     The minor axis is horizontal with a length of 8, so the semi-minor axis length is:
     
     \[
     b = \frac{8}{2} = 4
     \]

3. **Substitute the known values into the standard equation:**
   
   Plugging \( h = 3 \), \( k = -2 \), \( a^2 = 6^2 = 36 \), and \( b^2 = 4^2 = 16 \) into the standard form:
   
   \[
   \frac{(x - 3)^2}{16} + \frac{(y - (-2))^2}{36} = 1
   \]
   
   Simplifying further:
   
   \[
   \frac{(x - 3)^2}{16} + \frac{(y + 2)^2}{36} = 1
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{\frac{(x-3)^2}{16}+\frac{(y+2)^2}{36}=1}

To find the eccentricity of an ellipse given by the equation \( \frac{x^2}{36} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1 \), I first identify the values of \( a^2 \) and \( b^2 \). Here, \( a^2 = 36 \) which means \( a = 6 \), and \( b^2 = 9 \) so \( b = 3 \).

Next, since \( a > b \), the major axis is along the x-axis. The relationship between \( a \), \( b \), and the distance from the center to each focus (\( c \)) in an ellipse is given by:

\( c^2 = 36 - 9 = 27 \)

To find the eccentricity \( e \) of the ellipse given by the equation:

\[
\frac{x^2}{36} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1
\]

follow these steps:

1. **Identify the standard form of an ellipse:**

   The general equation of an ellipse centered at the origin is:
   
   \[
   \frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
   \]
   
   where \( a > b \) and the major axis is along the x-axis.

2. **Determine \( a^2 \) and \( b^2 \):**

   Comparing with the given equation:
   
   \[
   a^2 = 36 \quad \text{and} \quad b^2 = 9
   \]

   \]
   
4. **Calculate the distance from the center to each focus (\( c \)):**

To determine the equation of the ellipse with foci at \((40, 0)\) and \((-40, 0)\), and a major axis length of \(100\), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Identify Key Parameters
- **Foci**: The foci are located on the x-axis at \((40, 0)\) and \((-40, 0)\). This indicates that the center of the ellipse is at the origin \((0, 0)\), and the distance from the center to each focus (\(c\)) is \(40\).
- **Major Axis Length**: The length of the major axis is given as \(100\). Therefore, half of this length (the semi-major axis, \(a\)) is:
  \[
  a = \frac{100}{2} = 50
  \]

### Step 2: Determine the Semi-Minor Axis (\(b\))
For an ellipse, the relationship between \(a\), \(b\) (the semi-minor axis), and \(c\) is given by:
\[
a^2 = b^2 + c^2
\]
Plugging in the known values:
\[
50^2 = b^2 + 40^2 \\
2500 = b^2 + 1600 \\
b^2 = 900 \\
b = \sqrt{900} = 30
\]

### Step 3: Write the Equation of the Ellipse
The standard form of an ellipse centered at the origin with a horizontal major axis is:
\[
\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
\]
Substituting \(a = 50\) and \(b = 30\):
\[
\frac{x^2}{50^2} + \frac{y^2}{30^2} = 1 \\
\frac{x^2}{2500} + \frac{y^2}{900} = 1
\]

### Final Answer
The equation of the ellipse is:
\[
\boxed{\dfrac{x^{2}}{2500}+\dfrac{y^{2}}{900}=1}
\]

\]

we follow these steps:

The general form of a hyperbola centered at the origin with a vertical transverse axis is:

\[

\]

Now, let me find the vertices first. For a horizontal hyperbola, the vertices are located at (h ± a, k). Since h and k are zero, they should be at (a, 0) and (-a, 0). In our equation, under x² is 4, so a²=4 which means a=2. Therefore, the vertices are at (2,0) and (-2,0).

Next, I need to find the foci. The formula for c, where the foci are located at (h ± c, k), involves both a and b. I remember that c² = a² + b² for hyperbolas. Here, a² is 4 and under y² we have 9, so b²=9 which means b=3.

Calculating c: c² = 4 + 9 =13, so c=√13. Therefore, the foci are at (√13,0) and (-√13,0).

Now, for the asymptotes of a hyperbola. For horizontal hyperbolas, the equations are y = ±(b/a)x + k. Since the center is at (0,0), it simplifies to y=±(b/a)x. Substituting the values, b is 3 and a is 2, so the asymptotes are y=(3/2)x and y=(-3/2)x.

Let me just double-check everything. The vertices should be along the x-axis because it's horizontal. Yes, (2,0) and (-2,0). Foci further out on the same axis make sense since c is larger than a. And for asymptotes, with slope 3/2, that seems correct given b=3 and a=2.

I think I've covered all parts: vertices at (±a,0), foci at (±c,0), and asymptotes as y=±(b/a)x.
</think>

The hyperbola \( \frac{x^2}{4} - \frac{y^2}{9} = 1 \) is a standard horizontal hyperbola centered at the origin.

- **Vertices**: Located at \( (\pm 2, 0) \).
- **Foci**: Calculated using \( c = \sqrt{a^2 + b^2} = \sqrt{4 + 9} = \sqrt{13} \), so they are at \( (\pm \sqrt{13}, 0) \).
- **Asymptotes**: Given by the equations \( y = \pm \frac{b}{a}x = \pm \frac{3}{2}x \).
